"extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-OMNI/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "53a114a7567542c936f1d98cfc7dbba2315a0215ba18c1234470efa0abe01af6", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "--- PAGE 1 ---\n2025-03-27\nQwen2.5-Omni Technical Report\nQwen Team\nhttps://huggingface.co/Qwen\nhttps://modelscope.cn/organization/qwen\nhttps://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen2.5-Omni\nAbstract\nIn this report, we present Qwen2.5-Omni, an end-to-end multimodal model designed to\nperceive diverse modalities, including text, images, audio, and video, while simultane-\nously generating text and natural speech responses in a streaming manner. To enable\nthe streaming of multimodal information inputs, both audio and visual encoders utilize\na block-wise processing approach. This strategy effectively decouples the handling of\nlong sequences of multimodal data, assigning the perceptual responsibilities to the mul-\ntimodal encoder and entrusting the modeling of extended sequences to a large language\nmodel. Such a division of labor enhances the fusion of different modalities via the shared\nattention mechanism. To synchronize the timestamps of video inputs with audio, we\norganize the audio and video sequentially in an interleaved manner and propose a novel\nposition embedding approach, named TMRoPE (Time-aligned Multimodal RoPE). To\nconcurrently generate text and speech while avoiding interference between the two\nmodalities, we propose Thinker-Talker architecture. In this framework, Thinker func-\ntions as a large language model tasked with text generation, while Talker is a dual-track\nautoregressive model that directly utilizes the hidden representations from the Thinker to\nproduce audio tokens as output. Both the Thinker and Talker models are designed to be\ntrained and inferred in an end-to-end manner. For decoding audio tokens in a streaming\nmanner, we introduce a sliding-window DiT that restricts the receptive field, aiming\nto reduce the initial package delay. Qwen2.5-Omni is comparable with similarly sized\nQwen2.5-VL and outperforms Qwen2-Audio. Furthermore, Qwen2.5-Omni achieves\nstate-of-the-art performance on multimodal benchmarks like Omni-Bench. Notably,\nQwen2.5-Omni ’s performance in end-to-end speech instruction following is comparable\nto its capabilities with text inputs, as evidenced by benchmarks such as MMLU and\nGSM8K. As for speech generation, Qwen2.5-Omni’s streaming Talker outperforms most\nexisting streaming and non-streaming alternatives in robustness and naturalness.\nFigure 1: Qwen2.5-Omni is a unified end-to-end model capable of processing multiple modalities, such\nas text, audio, image and video, and generating real-time text or speech response. Based on these\nfeatures, Qwen2.5-Omni supports a wide range of tasks, including but not limited to voice dialogue,\nvideo dialogue, and video reasoning.\n1\narXiv:2503.20215v1  [cs.CL]  26 Mar 2025\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\n1\nIntroduction\nIn daily life, humans are capable of simultaneously perceiving the visual and auditory information\naround them. After processing this information through the brain, they express feedback through\nwriting, vocalization, or using tools (and physical actions), thereby engaging in information exchange\nwith various organisms in the world and exhibiting intelligence. In recent years, general artificial\nintelligence has become increasingly visible, largely due to advancements in Large Language Models\n(LLMs) (Brown et al., 2020; OpenAI, 2023; 2024; Gemini Team, 2024; Anthropic, 2023a;b; 2024; Bai et al.,\n2023a; Yang et al., 2024a; Touvron et al., 2023a;b; Dubey et al., 2024a). These models, trained on vast\namounts of textual data, represent high-level discrete representation created by humans, showcasing\nthe ability to solve complex problems and learn rapidly. Furthermore, in the realm of understanding,\nLanguage-Audio-Language Models (LALMs) (OpenAI, 2024; Tang et al., 2024; Chu et al., 2023b; 2024b)\nand Language-Visual-Language Models (LVLMs) (Li et al., 2023; Liu et al., 2023b; Dai et al., 2023; Zhu\net al., 2023; Huang et al., 2023; Bai et al., 2023b; Liu et al., 2023a; Wang et al., 2023b; OpenAI., 2023;\nGemini Team, 2024) have helped LLMs to further extend auditory and visual capabilities in an end-to-end\nmanner. However, efficiently unifying all these different understanding modalities in an end-to-end\nfashion, utilizing as much data as possible, and providing responses in both text and speech streams akin\nto human communication still presents a significant challenge.\nThe development of a unified and intelligent omni-model requires careful consideration of several key\nfactors. First, it is crucial to implement a systematic method for the joint training of various modalities,\nincluding text, images, videos, and audio, to foster mutual enhancement among them. This alignment\nis particularly important for video content, where synchronization of the temporal aspects of audio\nand visual signals is necessary. Second, it is essential to manage potential interference among outputs\nfrom different modalities, ensuring that the training processes for outputs such as text and voice tokens\ndo not disrupt each other. Finally, there is a need to explore architectural designs that enable real-\ntime understanding of multimodal information and allow for efficient audio output streaming, thereby\nreducing initial latency.\nIn this report, we introduce Qwen2.5-Omni, a unified single model capable of processing multiple\nmodalities and generating text and natural speech responses simultaneously in a streaming format. To\ntackle the first challenge, we propose a novel position embedding approach, named TMRoPE (Time-\naligned Multimodal RoPE). We organize these audio and video frames in an interleaved structure to\nrepresent video sequences in time order. For the second challenge, we present Thinker-Talker architecture,\nwherein Thinker is tasked with text generation while the Talker focuses on generating streaming speech\ntokens. Talker receives high-level representations directly from Thinker. This design is inspired by the\nway humans utilize different organs to produce various signals, which are simultaneously coordinated\nthrough the same neural networks. As a result, Thinker-Talker architecture is end-to-end jointly trained,\nwith each component dedicated to generating distinct signals. To address the challenges associated with\nstreaming and to facilitate the pre-filling necessary for real-time comprehension of multimodal signals,\nwe propose modifications to all multimodal encoders by adopting a block-wise streaming processing\napproach. In order to support streaming speech generation, we implement a dual-track autoregressive\nmodel that generates speech tokens, alongside a DiT model which converts these tokens into waveforms,\nthereby enabling streaming audio generation and minimizing initial latency. This design aims to enable\nthe model to process multimodal information in real-time and effectively perform pre-filling, thereby\nenabling the concurrent generation of text and speech signals.\nQwen2.5-Omni is comparable with the similarly sized Qwen2.5-VL (Wang et al., 2024c) and outperforms\nQwen2-Audio (Chu et al., 2024b) in image and audio capabilities respectively. Furthermore, Qwen2.5-\nOmni achieves state-of-the-art performance on multimodal benchmarks such as OmniBench (Li et al.,\n2024b) and AV-Odyssey Bench (Gong et al., 2024). Notably, Qwen2.5-Omni’s performance in end-to-\nend speech instruction following is comparable to its capabilities with text inputs, as evidenced by\nbenchmarks such as MMLU (Hendrycks et al., 2021a) and GSM8K (Cobbe et al., 2021). As for speech\ngeneration, Qwen2.5-Omni achieves 1.42%, 2.33% and 6.54% WER on seed-tts-eval (Anastassiou et al.,\n2024) test-zh, test-en and test-hard set respectively, outperforming MaskGCT (Wang et al., 2024e) and\nCosyVoice 2 (Du et al., 2024).\nThe key features of Qwen2.5-Omni can be summarized as:\n• We introduce Qwen2.5-Omni, a unified model that can perceive all modalities and simultaneously\ngenerate text and natural speech responses in a streaming fashion.\n• We present a novel positional embedding algorithm, termed TMRoPE, which explicitly incorpo-\nrates temporal information for synchronizing audio and video.\n• We propose the Thinker-Talker Architecture to facilitate real-time comprehension and speech\ngeneration.\n2\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\n• Qwen2.5-Omni demonstrates strong performance across all modalities when benchmarked\nagainst similarly sized single-modality models. It significantly enhances the capability of follow-\ning voice commands, achieving performance levels comparable to pure text input. For tasks that\ninvolve integrating multiple modalities, such as those evaluated in OmniBench (Li et al., 2024b),\nQwen2.5-Omni achieves state-of-the-art performance. Notably, Qwen2.5-Omni achieves strong\nperformance on seed-tts-eval (Anastassiou et al., 2024), demonstrating robust speech generation\nabilities.\n2\nArchitecture\nFigure 2: The overview of Qwen2.5-Omni. Qwen2.5-Omni adpots the Thinker-Talker architecture.\nThinker is tasked with text generation while Talker focuses on generating streaming speech tokens by\nreceives high-level representations directly from Thinker.\n2.1\nOverview\nAs shown in Figure 2, Qwen2.5-Omni employs Thinker-Talker architecture. Thinker functions like\na brain, responsible for processing and understanding inputs from text, audio and video modalities,\ngenerating high-level representations and corresponding text. Talker operates like a human mouth,\ntaking in the high-level representations and text produced by the Thinker in a streaming manner, and\noutputting discrete tokens of speech fluidly. Thinker is a Transformer decoder, accompanied by encoders\nfor audio and image that facilitate information extraction. In contrast, Talker is designed as a dual-track\nautoregressive Transformer Decoder architecture, motivated by Mini-Omni (Xie & Wu, 2024). During\nboth training and inference, Talker directly receives high-dimensional representations from Thinker and\nshares all of Thinker’s historical context information. Consequently, the entire architecture operates as a\ncohesive single model, enabling end-to-end training and inference.\nIn the following sections, we first introduce how Qwen2.5-Omni perceives various input signals and\npresent our proposed novel positional encoding algorithm, TMRoPE. Subsequently, the details of text\nand speech generation are presented. Finally, we highlight the improvements made in the understanding\nand generation modules to facilitate efficient streaming inference.\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\n2.2\nPerceivation\nFigure 3: An illustration of Time-aligned Multimodal RoPE (TMRoPE).\nText, Audio, Image and Video (w/o Audio).\nThinker processes text, audio, images, and video (without\nthe audio track) by converting them into a series of hidden representations for input. For tokenizing\ntext, we use Qwen’s tokenizer (Yang et al., 2024a), which applies byte-level byte-pair encoding with\na vocabulary comprising 151,643 regular tokens. Regarding audio input and audio from videos, we\nresample it to a frequency of 16kHz and transform the raw waveform into a 128-channel mel-spectrogram\nwith a window size of 25ms and a hop size of 10ms. We adopt the audio encoder from Qwen2-Audio (Chu\net al., 2024b), to make each frame of audio representation roughly corresponds to a 40ms segment of\nthe original audio signal. Furthermore, we employ the vision encoder from Qwen2.5-VL (Bai et al.,\n2025), which is based on the Vision Transformer (ViT) model with approximately 675 million parameters,\nenabling it to effectively handle both image and video inputs. The vision encoder employs a mixed\ntraining regimen incorporating both image and video data, ensuring proficiency in image understanding\nand video comprehension. To preserve video information as completely as possible while adapting to the\naudio sampling rate, we sample the video using a dynamic frame rate. Additionally, for consistency, each\nimage is treated as two identical frames.\nVideo and TMRoPE.\nWe propose a time-interleaving algorithm for audio and video, along with a novel\nposition encoding approach. As shown in Figure 3, TMRoPE encodes the 3-D positional information\nof multimodal inputs, which is Multimodal Rotary Position Embedding (M-RoPE) (Bai et al., 2023b)\nwith absolute temporal positions. This is achieved by deconstructing the original rotary embedding\ninto three components: temporal, height, and width. For text inputs, these components utilize identical\nposition IDs, making M-RoPE functionally equivalent to 1D-RoPE. Similarly, for audio inputs, we also\nuse identical position IDs and introduce absolute temporal position encoding, with one temporal ID\ncorresponding to 40ms.\nWhen processing images, the temporal IDs of each visual token remain constant, while distinct IDs are\nassigned to the height and width components based on the token’s position in the image. When the\ninput is video with audio, the audio is still encoded with identical position IDs for every 40ms per frame,\nand the video is treated as a series of images with temporal ID increments for each frame, while the\nheight and width components follow the same ID assignment pattern as images. Since the frame rate\nin video is not fixed, we dynamically adjust the temporal IDs between frames based on the actual time\ncorresponding to each frame to ensure that one temporal ID corresponds to 40ms. In scenarios where the\nmodel’s input encompasses multiple modalities, position numbering for each modality is initialized by\nincrementing the maximum position ID of the preceding modality by one. TMRoPE enhances positional\ninformation modeling, maximizing the integration of various modalities, enabling Qwen2.5-Omni to\nsimultaneously understand and analyze information from multiple modalities.\nAfter incorporating positional information into each modality, we arrange the representations in order. To\nenable the model to receive both visual and auditory information simultaneously, as shown in Figure 3,\nwe have a special design for video with audio called the time-interleaving method, which segments the\nrepresentation in the video with audio into chunks every 2 seconds according to the actual time. We\nthen arrange the visual representation at the front and the audio representation at the back within the 2\nseconds, interleaving the representations of the video with audio.\n2.3\nGeneration\nText.\nText is generated directly by Thinker. The logic of text generation is fundamentally the same as\nthat employed by widely used LLMs, which generate text through autoregressive sampling based on the\n4\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nprobability distribution over the vocabulary. The generation process may incorporate techniques such as\nrepetition penalty and top-p sampling to enhance its diversity.\nSpeech.\nTalker receives both high-level representations and embeddings of the text tokens sampled by\nThinker. The integration of high-dimensional representations and discrete sampling tokens is essential in\nthis context. As a streaming algorithm, voice generation must anticipate the content’s tone and attitude\nbefore the entire text is fully generated. The high-dimensional representations provided by Thinker\nimplicitly convey this information, enabling a more natural streaming generation process. Furthermore,\nThinker’s representations primarily express semantic similarity in the representational space rather than\nphonetic similarity. Consequently, even phonetically distinct words may have very similar high-level\nrepresentations, necessitating the input of sampled discrete tokens to eliminate such uncertainty.\nWe designed an efficient speech codec named qwen-tts-tokenizer. qwen-tts-tokenizer efficiently represents\nkey information of speech and can be decoded to speech streamingly through a causal audio decoder.\nAfter receiving the information, Talker starts to autoregressively generate audio tokens and text tokens.\nThe generation of speech does not require word-level and timestamp-level alignment with the text. This\nsignificantly simplifies the requirements for training data and the inference process.\n2.4\nDesigns for Streaming\nIn the context of streaming audio and video interactions, the initial packet latency is a critical indicator of\nthe system’s streaming performance. This latency is influenced by several factors: 1) the delay caused\nby the processing of multimodal information inputs; 2) the latency from the moment the first text\ninput is received until the first voice token is output; 3) the delay in converting the first segment of\nspeech into audio; and 4) the inherent latency of the architecture itself, which is related to model size,\ncomputational FLOPs, and other factors. This paper will subsequently discuss the algorithmic and\narchitectural improvements made to reduce these latencies across these four dimensions.\nSupport Prefilling.\nChunked-prefills is a mechanism widely used in modern inference framework. To\nsupport it in modalities interation, we modified the audio and visual encoders to support block-wise\nattention along the temporal dimension. Specifically, the audio encoder is changed from full attention\nover the entire audio to performing attention in blocks of 2 seconds each. The vision encoder utilizes flash\nattention for efficient training and inference with a simple MLP layer that merges adjacent 2×2 tokens\ninto a single token. The patch size is set to 14, which allows images of different resolutions to be packed\ninto a sequence.\nStreaming Codec Generation.\nTo facilitate the streaming of audio, especially for extended sequences,\nwe propose a sliding window block attention mechanism that restricts the current token’s access to a\nlimited context. Specifically, we utilize a Flow-Matching (Lipman et al.) DiT model. The input code is\ntransformed into a mel-spectrogram using Flow-Matching, followed by a modified BigVGAN (Lee et al.)\nto reconstruct the generated mel-spectrogram back into the waveform.\nFigure 4: An illustration of sliding window block attention mechanism in DiT for codec to wav generation.\nAs shown in Figure 4, to generate waveforms from code, we group adjacent codes into blocks and use\nthese for our attention mask. We limit the DiT’s receptive field to 4 blocks, including a lookback of 2\nblocks and a lookahead of 1 block. During decoding, we generate the mel-spectrum in chunks using Flow\nMatching, ensuring that each code chunk has access to the necessary contextual blocks. This approach\nenhances the quality of streaming outputs by maintaining contextual information. We also use this\nchunk-by-chunk method for BigVGAN’s fixed receptive field to facilitate streaming waveform generation\n5\n\n\n--- PAGE 6 ---\n3\nPre-training\nQwen2.5-Omni consists of three training stages. In the first stage, we lock the LLM parameters and focus\nexclusively on training the vision encoder and audio encoder, utilizing a vast corpus of audio-text and\nimage-text pairs to enhance semantic understanding within the LLM. In the second stage, we unfreeze\nall parameters and train with a wider range of multimodal data for more comprehensive learning. In\nthe final stage, we use data with a sequence length of 32k to enhance the model’s ability to understand\ncomplex long-sequence data.\nThe model is pre-trained on a diverse dataset that includes various types such as image-text, video-text,\nvideo-audio, audio-text and text corpus. We replace the hierarchical tags with the natural language\nprompts following Qwen2-Audio (Chu et al., 2024a), which can improve better generalization ability and\nbetter instruction following ability.\nDuring the initial pre-training phase, the LLM component of Qwen2.5-Omni is initialized using the\nparameters from Qwen2.5 (Yang et al., 2024b), while the vision encoder is the same as Qwen2.5-VL, and\nthe audio encoder is initialized with the Whisper-large-v3 (Radford et al., 2023). The two encoders are\ntrained separately on the fixed LLM, with both initially focusing on training their respective adapters\nbefore training the encoders. This foundational training is crucial in equipping the model with a robust\nunderstanding of core visual-textual and audio-textual correlations and alignments.\nThe second phase of pre-training marks a significant advancement by incorporating an additional 800\nbillion tokens of image and video related data, 300 billion tokens of audio related data, and 100 billion\ntokens of video with audio related data. This phase introduces a larger volume of mixed multimodal data\nand a wider variety of tasks, which enhances the interaction and deepens the understanding between\nauditory, visual, and textual information. The inclusion of multimodal, multitask datasets is crucial for\ndeveloping the model’s ability to handle multiple tasks and modalities simultaneously, a vital capability\nfor managing complex real-world datasets. Moreover, pure text data plays an essential role in maintaining\nand improving language proficiency.\nTo improve training efficiency, we limited the maximum token length to 8192 tokens in the previous\nstages. Then, we incorporat long audio and long video data and extend the original text, audio, image,\nand video data to 32,768 tokens for training. Experimental results indicate that our data shows significant\nimprovement in supporting long sequence data\n4\nPost-training\n4.1\nData Format\nThe Dataset Format Example of ChatML\n<|im_start|>user\n<|vision_start|>Video.mp4 [Two people are talking in the video]<|vision_end|>What are the\npeople in the video saying?<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>assistant\nBoth pictures are of SpongeBob SquarePants.The person in the red clothes says, \"Hello, how’s\nthe weather today?\" The person in the black clothes responds, \"Hello, the weather is quite nice\ntoday.\"<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>user\n<|vision_start|>Video.mp4 [A person in the video is saying, \"Please describe the person in front\nof you.\"]<|vision_end|><|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>assistant\nThe person in front of you is wearing glasses and a brown jacket over a blue shirt. They appear\nto be speaking or reacting to something, as their mouth is open and they seem engaged. The\nbackground shows a room with a wall-mounted air conditioner, a clothing rack with various\ngarments hanging on it, and a large screen displaying an image of a cityscape at night. The\nlighting in the room is warm and cozy.<|im_end|>\n4.2\nThinker\nDuring the post-training phase, we employ instruction-following data with ChatML (OpenAI, 2022)\nformat for instruction-finetuning. Our dataset incorporates pure text-based dialogue data, visual-modality\nconversation data, audio-modality conversation data and mix-modality conversation data.\n6\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\n4.3\nTalker\nWe introduced a three-stage training process for Talker, allowing Qwen2.5-Omni to generate text and\nspeech responses simultaneously. In the first stage, we train Talker to learn context continuation. The\nsecond stage utilized DPO (Rafailov et al., 2023) to enhance the stability of speech generation. In the third\nstage, we applied multi-speaker instruction fine-tuning to improve the naturalness and controllability of\nthe speech responses.\nDuring the In-Context Learning (ICL) training phase, in addition to utilizing text supervision similar\nto that of Thinker, we perform a speech continuation task through next-token prediction, leveraging\nan extensive dataset of dialogues that incorporate multimodal contexts and spoken responses. Talker\nlearns to establish a monotonic mapping from semantic representation to speech, while also acquiring\nthe ability to express speech with diverse attributes that are contextually appropriate, such as prosody,\nemotion, and accent. Additionally, we implement timbre disentanglement techniques to prevent the\nmodel from associating specific voices with infrequent textual patterns.\nLDPO(Pθ; Pref) = −E(x,yw,yl)∼D\n\u0014\nlog σ\n\u0012\nβ log Pθ(yw | x)\nPref(yw | x) −β log Pθ(yl | x)\nPref(yl | x)\n\u0013\u0015\n.\n(1)\nTo broaden the coverage of speakers and scenarios, the pretraining data inevitably contains label noise and\npronunciation errors, leading to model hallucinations. To mitigate this issue, we introduce a reinforcement\nlearning phase to improve the stability of speech generation. Specifically, for each request and response\ntext paired with the reference speech, we build a dataset D with the triplet data (x, yw, yl), where x is\nthe input sequence with input text, and yw and yl are the good and bad generated speech sequences\nrespectively. We rank these samples based on their reward scores associated with word error rate (WER)\nand the punctuation pause error rate.\nLastly, we performed speaker fine-tuning on the aforementioned base model, enabling Talker to adopt\nspecific voices and improve its naturalness.\n5\nEvaluation\nWe conduct comprehensive evaluation of Qwen2.5-Omni. The model is divided into two main categories:\nunderstanding (X→Text) and speech generation (X→Speech).\n5.1\nEvaluation of X→Text\nIn this section, we evaluate Qwen2.5-Omni’s ability to comprehend various multimodal inputs (text,\naudio, image, and video) and generate textual responses.\nText→Text\nOur evaluation of Qwen2.5-Omni on text →text primarily focuses on general evaluation,\nmathematics & science ability and coding ability. Specifically, we utilize MMLU-Pro (Wang et al.,\n2024f), MMLU-redux (Gema et al., 2024) and Livebench0803 (White et al., 2024) for general evaluation,\nGPQA (Rein et al., 2023), GSM8K (Cobbe et al., 2021) and MATH (Hendrycks et al., 2021b) for mathematics\n& science, HumanEval (Chen et al., 2021), MBPP (Austin et al., 2021), MultiPL-E (Cassano et al., 2023)\nand LiveCodeBench 2305-2409 (Jain et al., 2024) for coding.\nAudio→Text\nThe evaluation of Qwen2.5-Omni for audio →text includes audio understanding, audio\nreasoning, and voice-chatting. Specifically, we perform a comprehensive evaluation on Automatic\nSpeech Recognition (ASR), Speech-to-Text Translation (S2TT), Speech Entity Recognition (SER), Vocal\nSound classification (VSC) and Music, which assesses the performance of Qwen2.5-Omni on a broad\nrange of audio understanding tasks. We utilize MMAU (Sakshi et al., 2024) for audio reasoning tasks,\nVoiceBench (Chen et al., 2024b) and a self-curated speech-instruction benchmark for voice-chatting tasks.\nImage→Text\nThe evaluation of Qwen2.5-Omni for image →text primarily emphasizes the performance\nin college-level problems, math, general visual question answering and OCR-related tasks. Specifically, we\nutilize MMMU (Yue et al., 2023) and MMMU-Pro (Yue et al., 2024) for college-level problems evaluation,\nMathVista (Lu et al., 2024b) and MathVision (Wang et al., 2024b) for math. For general visual question\nanswering, we evaluate the performance on benchmark datasets such as MMBench-V1.1 (Liu et al.,\n2023c), MMVet (Yu et al., 2024), MMStar (Chen et al., 2024a), MME (Fu et al., 2023), MuirBench (Wang\net al., 2024a), CRPE (Wang et al., 2024d), RealWorldQA (X.AI., 2024), MMERealWorld (Zhang et al., 2024),\nand MM-MT-Bench (Agrawal et al., 2024). Additionally, we evaluate Qwen2.5-Omni on various OCR\nbenchmarks, such as AI2D (Kembhavi et al., 2016), TextVQA (Singh et al., 2019), DocVQA (Mathew\n7\n\n\n--- PAGE 8 ---\net al., 2021), ChartQA (Masry et al., 2022), and OCRBench_v2 (Fu et al., 2024b). Furthermore, we\nalso evaluate the visual grounding capability of our model on the referring expression comprehension\nbenchmarks (Kazemzadeh et al., 2014; Mao et al., 2016), object detection in the wild (Li et al., 2022) and a\nself-curated point grounding benchmark.\nVideo (w/o Audio)→Text\nWe assess our model on several representative video understanding tasks\nlike Video-MME (Fu et al., 2024a), MVBench (Li et al., 2024a), and EgoSchema (Mangalam et al., 2023).\nMultimodality→Text\nWe demonstrate the ability of our model for mixed-modality (image, audio and\ntext) prompts on OmniBench (Li et al., 2024b).\n5.1.1\nPerformance of Text→Text\nWe compare Qwen2.5-Omni with other leading large language model of similar size (7B). As shown in\nTable 1, the performance of Qwen2.5-Omni generally falls between Qwen2-7B and Qwen2.5-7B. Our\nmodel outperforms Qwen2-7B on most benchmarks, such as MMLU-Pro, MMLU-redux, MATH, GSM8K,\nMBPP, MultiPL-E and LiveCodeBench, which demonstrates the exceptional capabilities of our model for\nText→Text.\nTable 1: Text →Text performance of 7B+ pure text models and Qwen2.5-Omni\nDatasets\nGemma2-9B Llama3.1-8B Qwen2-7B Qwen2.5-7B Qwen2.5-Omni-7B\nGeneral Tasks\nMMLU-Pro\n52.1\n48.3\n44.1\n56.3\n47.0\nMMLU-redux\n72.8\n67.2\n67.3\n75.4\n71.0\nLiveBench0831\n30.6\n26.7\n29.2\n35.9\n29.6\nMathematics & Science Tasks\nGPQA\n32.8\n32.8\n34.3\n36.4\n30.8\nMATH\n44.3\n51.9\n52.9\n75.5\n71.5\nGSM8K\n76.7\n84.5\n85.7\n91.6\n88.7\nCoding Tasks\nHumanEval\n68.9\n72.6\n79.9\n84.8\n78.7\nMBPP\n74.9\n69.6\n67.2\n79.2\n73.2\nMultiPL-E\n53.4\n50.7\n59.1\n70.4\n65.8\nLiveCodeBench2305-2409\n18.9\n8.3\n23.9\n28.7\n24.6\n5.1.2\nPerformance of Audio→Text\nWe compare Qwen2.5-Omni with other leading specialist or generalist models on diverse audio under-\nstanding, audio reasoning, and voice-chatting benchmarks. As shown in Table 2 and 3, Qwen2.5-Omni\ndelivers better or comparable performance with other state-of-the-art methods on audio understanding.\nFor instance, it achieves superior ASR and S2TT performance on Fleurs_zh, CommonVoice_en, Com-\nmonVoice_zh, CoVoST2_en-de and CoVoST2_zh-en test sets, surpassing previous state-of-the-art models\nlike Whisper-large-v3, Qwen2Audio, MinMo and other Omni models. Qwen2.5-Omni also achieves\nstate-of-the-art performance on general audio understanding tasks like music and VSC. Additionally,\nQwen2.5-Omni achieves state-of-the-art results on audio reasoning with superior performance on sound,\nmusic and speech subsets of MMAU benchmark. These results demonstrate the powerful capabilities of\nQwen2.5-Omni in general audio understanding and reasoning.\nAdditionally, on VoiceBench, Qwen2.5-Omni achieves an impressive average score of 74.12, surpassing\nother audio language models and omni models of similar size. This showcases our model’s strong\ncapabilities in speech interaction. To further explore the performance of diverse speech interaction, we\nconvert text instructions from several pure-text benchmarks into speech and evaluate Qwen2.5-Omni,\nQwen2-Audio and Qwen2-7B on the in-house voice-chat benchmark. About 90% of text-instructions\nare utilized. We use speech instruction for Qwen2.5-Omni and Qwen2-Audio, and text instruction for\nQwen2-7B. As shown in Table 4, compared to Qwen2-Audio, Qwen2.5-Omni significantly narrowes\nthe gap with Qwen2-7B, which uses text instructions. Due to the lack of\nrelevant assessments, the evaluation of speech generation focuses primarily speech generation given\ntexts, similarity to text-to-speech (TTS), on two aspects: Zero-shot and Single-Speaker speech generation\ncapabilities.\n• Zero-Shot Speech Generation We assessed the content consistency (WER) and speaker similarity\n(SIM) of our model in zero-shot speech generation on SEED (Anastassiou et al., 2024).\n• Single-Speaker Speech Generation We assessed the stability of our speaker fine-tuned model\non the SEED (Anastassiou et al., 2024), and evaluated the subjective naturalness (NMOS) of the\ngenerated speech on a self-created dataset.\n5.2.1\nEvaluation of Zero-Shot Speech Generation.\nWe compared the Qwen2.5-Omni with state-of-the-art zero-shot TTS systems. As shown in Table 9,\nQwen2.5-Omni demonstrates highly competitive performance, highlighting its robust speech under-\nstanding and generation capabilities developed through in-context learning (ICL). Additionally, after\nreinforcement learning (RL) optimization, Qwen2.5-Omni showed significant improvements in genera-\ntion stability, with marked reductions in attention misalignment, pronunciation errors, and inappropriate\npauses on the challenging test-hard dataset.\n5.2.2\nEvaluation of Single-Speaker Speech Generation.\nWe compared the Qwen2.5-Omni model before and after speaker fine-tuning, as well as with human\nrecordings. As shown in Table 10, the speaker-finetuned Qwen2.5-Omni more precisely captured the\nnuanced prosodic styles of the target speakers while preserving the foundational stability provided by\nthe base model, achieving performance that approaches human-level quality across both subjective and\nobjective metrics.\n6\nConclusion\nQwen2.5-Omni is a unified model designed to understand and generate multiple modalities, including\ntext and real-time speech. To enhance video integration, we’ve introduced a new positional embedding\nmethod called TMRoPE, which aligns audio and video timing. Our Thinker-Talker framework supports\nreal-time speech generation while minimizing interference across different modalities. Additionally, we\nemploy techniques such as block-wise audio/vision encoding and a sliding window mechanism for code-\nto-wav generation. This innovative model excels in complex audio-visual interactions and emotional\n12\n\n\n--- PAGE 13 ---\nTable 9: Zero-Shot Speech Generation\nDatasets\nModel\nPerformance\nContent Consistency\nSEED\ntest-zh | test-en |\ntest-hard\nSeed-TTSICL (Anastassiou et al., 2024) 1.11 | 2.24 | 7.58\nSeed-TTSRL (Anastassiou et al., 2024)\n1.00 | 1.94 | 6.42\nMaskGCT (Wang et al., 2024e)\n2.27 | 2.62 | 10.27\nE2 TTS (Eskimez et al., 2024)\n1.97 | 2.19 | -\nF5-TTS (Chen et al., 2024c)\n1.56 | 1.83 | 8.67\nCosyVoice 2 (Du et al., 2024)\n1.45 | 2.57 | 6.83\nCosyVoice 2-S (Du et al., 2024)\n1.45 | 2.38 | 8.08\nQwen2.5-Omni-7BICL\n1.70 | 2.72 | 7.97\nQwen2.5-Omni-7BRL\n1.42 | 2.33 | 6.54\nSpeaker Similarity\nSEED\ntest-zh | test-en |\ntest-hard\nSeed-TTSICL (Anastassiou et al., 2024) 0.796 | 0.762 | 0.776\nSeed-TTSRL (Anastassiou et al., 2024)\n0.801 | 0.766 | 0.782\nMaskGCT (Wang et al., 2024e)\n0.774 | 0.714 | 0.748\nE2 TTS (Eskimez et al., 2024)\n0.730 | 0.710 | -\nF5-TTS (Chen et al., 2024c)\n0.741 | 0.647 | 0.713\nCosyVoice 2 (Du et al., 2024)\n0.748 | 0.652 | 0.724\nCosyVoice 2-S (Du et al., 2024)\n0.753 | 0.654 | 0.732\nQwen2.5-Omni-7BICL\n0.752 | 0.632 | 0.747\nQwen2.5-Omni-7BRL\n0.754 | 0.641 | 0.752\nTable 10: Single-Speaker Speech Generation\nDatasets\nModel\nPerformance\nContent Consistency\nSEED\ntest-zh | test-en |\ntest-hard\nHuman\n1.25 | 2.14 | -\nQwen2.5-Omni RL\n1.30 | 2.33 | 6.54\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker A 1.29 | 1.86 | 6.59\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker B 1.37 | 1.89 | 7.25\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker C 1.30 | 2.13 | 6.43\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker D 1.28 | 1.83 | 7.16\nNaturalness\nNMOS\nzh | en\nHuman\n4.51 | -\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker A\n4.46 | 4.51\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker B\n4.51 | 4.62\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker C\n4.50 | 4.60\nQwen2.5-Omni Speaker D\n4.48 | 4.58\ncontext in speech dialogues. Comprehensive evaluations show that Qwen2.5-Omni outperforms similarly\nsized single-modality models, particularly in following voice commands, and achieves state-of-the-art\nperformance in multi-modal tasks.\nIn the development of the model, we have identified several critical issues that have often been over-\nlooked by researchers in previous academic studies, such as video OCR and audio-video collaborative\nunderstanding. Addressing these challenges necessitates collaboration between the academic and indus-\ntrial sectors, particularly in building comprehensive evaluation benchmarks and research datasets. We\nbelieve Qwen2.5-Omni represents a significant advancement toward artificial general intelligence (AGI).\nOur future goals include developing a more robust and faster model with expanded output capabilities\nacross various modalities like images, videos, and music.\n7\nAuthors\nCore Contributors: Jin Xu, Zhifang Guo, Jinzheng He, Hangrui Hu, Ting He, Shuai Bai, Keqin Chen,\nJialin Wang, Yang Fan, Kai Dang, Bin Zhang, Xiong Wang, Yunfei Chu, Junyang Lin\n13\n\n\n--- PAGE 14 ---\nContributors1: An Yang, Anfeng Li, Baosong Yang, Bei Chen, Bin Lin, Binyuan Hui, Bo Zheng, Bowen\nYu, Cheng Chen, Chengen Huang, Chenhan Yuan, Chengyuan Li, Daren Chen, Dayiheng Liu, Dake Guo,\nFan Zhou, Fei Huang, Guangdong Zhou, Hang Zhang, Haoran Lian, Haoyang Zhang, He Wang, Humen\nZhong, Jian Yang, Jiandong Jiang, Jianhong Tu, Jianqiang Wan, Jianyuan Zeng, Jun Tang, Jianwei Zhang,\nJianxin Yang, Jianyuan Zeng, Jing Zhou, Jingren Zhou, Kexin Yang, Lei Xie, Linhan Ma, Lingchen Meng,\nLe Yu, Mei Li, Miao Hong, Mingfeng Xue, Mingkun Yang, Mingze Li, Na Ni, Pei Zhang, Peiyang Zhang,\nPeng Liu, Peng Wang, Peng Zhang, Pengfei Wang, Rui Hu, Rui Men, Qiuyue Wang, Qing Fu, Shixuan\nLiu, Sibo Song, Siqi Zhang, Song Chen, Tianyi Tang, Tao He, Ting He, Wenbin Ge, Wei Ding, Xiaodong\nDeng, Xinyao Niu, Xipin Wei, Xue Bin, Xuejing Liu, Xingzhang Ren, Xuancheng Ren, Yang Liu, Yanpeng\nLi, Yang Liu, Yang Su, Yichang Zhang, Yuqiong Liu, Yuanjun Lv, Yuanzhi Zhu, Yuxuan Cai, Zeyu Cui,\nZheng Li, Zhenru Zhang, Zihan Qiu, Zhaohai Li, Zhibo Yang, Zhipeng Zhou, Zhiyuan Zhu\nReferences\nPravesh Agrawal, Szymon Antoniak, Emma Bou Hanna, Baptiste Bout, Devendra Chaplot, Jessica\nChudnovsky, Diogo Costa, Baudouin De Monicault, Saurabh Garg, Theophile Gervet, Soham Ghosh,\nAmélie Héliou, Paul Jacob, Albert Q. Minigpt-4: Enhancing vision-\nlanguage understanding with advanced large language models. arXiv:2304.10592, 2023.\n20\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 71173]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 71173, "selected_characters": 71173, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-MOONSHINE/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "e0fd9d8b31bccf8c41eee0c181bcfb00cf89461a7e0b80844984e7b4cf0d19eb", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "--- PAGE 1 ---\nMoonshine: Speech Recognition for Live Transcription and Voice Commands\nNat Jeffries Evan King Manjunath Kudlur Guy Nicholson James Wang Pete Warden\nUseful Sensors\nAbstract\nThis paper introduces Moonshine, a family of\nspeech recognition models optimized for live tran-\nscription and voice command processing. Moon-\nshine is based on an encoder-decoder transformer\narchitecture and employs Rotary Position Em-\nbedding (RoPE) instead of traditional absolute\nposition embeddings. The model is trained on\nspeech segments of various lengths, but with-\nout using zero-padding, leading to greater ef-\nficiency for the encoder during inference time.\nWhen benchmarked against OpenAI’s Whisper\ntiny.en, Moonshine Tiny demonstrates a 5x re-\nduction in compute requirements for transcribing\na 10-second speech segment while incurring no in-\ncrease in word error rates across standard evalua-\ntion datasets. These results highlight Moonshine’s\npotential for real-time and resource-constrained\napplications.\n1. Introduction\nReal-time automatic speech recognition (ASR) is essential\nfor many applications, including live transcription during\npresentations, accessibility tools for individuals with hear-\ning impairments, and voice command processing for conver-\nsational interfaces in smart devices and wearables. These\napplications often run directly on low-cost hardware, where\nstrict resource constraints and a lack of internet connectivity\nintroduce unique technical challenges that are not present in\nother ASR domains.\nThe introduction of OpenAI’s Whisper has led to a signifi-\ncant improvement in the accuracy of general-purpose ASR\nsystems (Radford et al., 2022). However, one of the pri-\nmary challenges in applications of on-device ASR is the\nneed to minimize latency—the time delay between a spoken\nutterance and the corresponding text appearing, e.g., on a\nlive transcription display—without losing accuracy. During\n.\nCorrespondence\nto:\nManjunath\nKudlur\n<keve-\nman@usefulsensors.com>.\nour development of one such application—a Caption Box\nbuilt to provide rapid, accurate, and private offline transcrip-\ntion of English speech—we found that current models are\nill-suited to the task. When deployed on a low-cost ARM-\nbased processor, we noticed that even the smallest Whisper\ntiny.en model had a firm lower latency bound of 500\nmilliseconds, irrespective of the duration of audio. User\nfeedback indicated that this level of latency failed to provide\na smooth and responsive user experience, which motivated\nus to investigate deeper.\nWhisper, which is based on an encoder-decoder transformer\narchitecture, processes fixed-length audio sequences in 30-\nsecond chunks regardless of the actual speech content of\nthe audio. This means that shorter audio sequences require\npadding with zeros to meet the length requirement, resulting\nin a constant computational overhead in the encoder. While\nthe decoder’s processing time is proportional to the length\nof the utterance, the constant overhead of fixed-length en-\ncoding places a firm lower bound to latency—e.g., the 500\nmilliseconds we identified during our own testing with the\nCaption Box. Our initial attempts to remove this bottle-\nneck involved fine-tuning and distilling 1 Whisper models\nto handle variable-length sequences in the encoder, utilizing\nopen audio datasets. However, these open datasets proved\ninsufficient to surpass Whisper’s Word Error Rate (WER).\nRecognizing the limits of available data and the opportunity\nto leverage recent advancements in model architectures, we\nopted to develop and train new models from scratch.\nOur first task was to better quantify the bottleneck imposed\nby Whisper’s fixed-length encoder. In an empirical test,\nwe compared the GFLOPS (i.e., billion floating-point op-\nerations) needed for processing 30 seconds of zero-padded\naudio to the GFLOPS that would be required to instead pro-\ncess only a < 30 second subset of the same audio—i.e., we\nmeasured the potential for speed-ups if Whisper instead had\na variable-length encoder. Our results (Figure 1) showed\npotential for a 35x speed-up in the best case, and nearly a\n5x speed-up overall. Clearly, improvements to Whisper’s\narchitecture could yield lightweight, low-latency speech-to-\n1Hence the name Moonshine. We progressed to not use distil-\nlation eventually, but the name stuck.\narXiv:2410.15608v2  [cs.SD]  22 Oct 2024\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nMoonshine: Speech Recognition for Live Transcription and Voice Commands\n2\n0\n5\n10\n15\n20\n25\n30\n0\n5\n10\n15\n20\nAbsolute\nFixed-length encoder\nVariable-length encoder\n0\n5\n10\n15\n20\n25\n30\nSeconds of audio\n2x\n5x\n10x\n20x\n40x\nSpeed-up (log scale)\nVariable-length encoder\nGFLOPS to decode\nFigure 1. GFLOPS required by Whisper tiny.en for decoding\nas a function of input audio duration. OpenAI’s Whisper models\nuse a fixed-length encoder that requires any audio less than 30\nseconds to be zero-padded; testing with a hypothetical variable-\nlength encoder shows that we can attain significant speed-ups by\nremoving this requirement.\ntext models that are better-suited to resource-constrained\napplications.\nToward tackling the challenges faced by on-device, low-\nlatency ASR applications, this paper introduces the Moon-\nshine family of ASR models. Moonshine models are de-\nsigned to match Whisper’s accuracy while optimizing com-\nputational efficiency by eliminating zero-padding require-\nments, instead scaling processing demands proportionally\nto audio input length. We describe Moonshine’s architec-\nture and training process, and provide a detailed analysis\nof its performance benefits in comparison with Whisper\nmodels. Section 2 motivates our development of Moon-\nshine by quantifying the WER when adapting Whisper for\nvariable-length audio. Section 3 describes the Moonshine\narchitecture, dataset preparation, and training process, while\nSection 4 provides an evaluation of the results on standard\nspeech recognition datasets. Section 5 concludes.\n2. Issues with fixed sequence length encoder\nWhisper models use absolute position embeddings in both\nthe encoder and decoder. The encoder in particular uses\nsinusoidal position embeddings of shape [1500, dim],\nwhere dim is the transformer encoder’s dimension. The\nstandard inference code provided by OpenAI zero-pads the\ninput audio to an exact 30-second segment before passing it\ndown to the model. Audio feature generation and 2 convolu-\ntion layers convert this audio segment to a [1500, dim]\nvector sequence that gets added to the sinusoidal position\nembedding. The left-most column of Figure 2 illustrates\nthis mechanism.\nRequiring an exact 30-second segment causes the encoder\nto incur a fixed computation cost, no matter the duration\nof actual speech in the audio segment. However, since\nWhisper’s model architecture is an off-the-shelf, variable-\nlength sequence-to-sequence transformer, zero-padding is\nnot strictly necessary. An audio segment that is shorter\nthan 30 seconds can indeed be processed through the\nmodel’s frontend to yield a [seq_length, dim] vector\nsequence where seq_length <= 1500.\nThere are several options for adding the position embedding\nto a variable-length audio input. We compared the default\nimplementation in OpenAI’s Whisper inference code (i.e.,\nusing zero-padding to form 30-second segments) with two\nother options, as illustrated in the center and right-most\ncolumns of Figure 2—namely, using the prefix and the\nsuffix of the position embedding. We computed the WER\nfor the tiny.en model using these two options on the\ntest.clean split of the Librispeech dataset. This split\nhas 2620 examples, 9 of which are more than 30 seconds in\nlength. We ignore these 9 examples in this evaluation. We\nuse a beam size of 5, so as to not suffer the pitfalls of greedy\ndecoding such as repetitions. With the standard inference\ncode provided by OpenAI, we get a WER of 5.21%. When\nthe input is not zero-padded, however, we get worse results.\nWhen using only a prefix of the position embedding, the\ntiny.en model has a WER of 107.38%. We notice a\nlot of repetitions towards the end of the transcription, even\nwith the beam search. When instead using the suffix of\nthe position embedding, the accuracy degradation is not as\ndramatic, producing a WER of 18.45%—still more than\nthree times worse than the baseline.\nThese results indicate that the OpenAI Whisper models\nare trained with examples that are all exactly 30 seconds\nlong. The models indeed produce high quality transcrip-\ntions for long-form audio. However, the fixed computation\nbudget needed for their encoder makes them inefficient for\nlow-latency applications, such as live transcription. Since\nthe audio segments in these applications tend to be short\nand/or vary in length, our effort to train models optimized\nfor variable-length sequences is well-motivated.\n3. Approach\nThis section describes Moonshine’s model architecture be-\nfore detailing our data collection, preprocessing, and model\ntraining process.\n3.1. Model architecture\nMoonshine is an off-the-shelf encoder-decoder Transformer\n(Vaswani et al., 2017) model. The input is an audio signal\nsampled at 16,000 Hz. We do not use any hand-engineering\n(e.g., Mel spectrograms) to extract audio features. Instead,\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\nMoonshine: Speech Recognition for Live Transcription and Voice Commands\n3\nx + positional_embedding[:,:]\nx + positional_embedding[:x.shape[1],:]\nx + positional_embedding[-x.shape[1]:,:]\n+\n+\n+\n5.21% WER\n107.38% WER\n18.45% WER\nZero-Padded\nTruncated (Prefix)\nTruncated (Suffix)\nFigure 2. Variations of absolute position embeddings usage and the corresponding WER produced by Whisper tiny.en (test.clean\nsplit of the Librispeech dataset). Simply adapting Whisper’s inference code to avoid encoding of fixed-length audio (as in the center and\nright-most columns) introduces significant increases in WER, motivating our development of new models with variable-length encoding.\nthe input is processed by a short stem of 3 convolution layers\nwith strides 64, 3, and 2 respectively. The filter widths,\nnumber of channels and activation functions are shown in\nFigure 3. These set of strides compress the input by a\nfactor of 384x. Note that in the Whisper model, the input is\ncompressed by 320x—firstly 160x in the Mel spectogram\ncomputation, then by a further 2x in the convolution stem.\nWe use Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) (Su et al., 2023)\nat each layer of the encoder and decoder. Table 1 compares\nMoonshine’s architecture with OpenAI’s Whisper models.\nWe use the same byte-level BPE text tokenizer used in Llama\n1 and 2 (Touvron et al., 2023; Sennrich et al., 2015) for\ntokenizing English text. The original vocabulary size is\n32000; we add 768 special tokens for future extensions.\n3.2. Training data collection & preprocessing\nWe train Moonshine on a combination of 90K hours\nfrom open ASR datasets and over 100K hours from own\ninternally-prepared dataset, totalling around 200K hours.\nFrom open datasets, we use Common Voice 16.1 (Ardila\net al., 2020), the AMI corpus (Carletta et al., 2005), Gi-\ngaSpeech (Chen et al., 2021), LibriSpeech (Panayotov\net al., 2015), the English subset of multilingual Lib-\nriSpeech (Pratap et al., 2020), and People’s Speech (Galvez\net al., 2021). We then augment this training corpus with\ndata that we collect from openly-available sources on the\nweb. We discuss preparation methods for our self-collected\ndata in the following.\nPreprocessing noisily-labeled speech.\nMany speech\nsources available on the web have subtitles or captions avail-\nable, which can serve as labels. However, captions tend be\nnoisy—they may be manually-generated and thus contain\ntext that is orthogonal to the audio content, or they may\ncontain the names of speakers or verbal descriptions of non-\nspeech content. In cases where a manually-generated but\npossibly-unreliable caption is available, we use a heuristic\nprocess to filter out low-quality instances. First, we lower-\ncase and normalize the caption text, removing or replacing,\ne.g., ambiguous unicode characters, emoji, and punctuation.\nWe then use Whisper large v3 to generate a pseudo-label\nof the audio content, applying the same text normalization\nto this pseudo-label as we do the caption. Finally, we com-\npute a normalized Levenshtein distance (between [0.0, 1.0],\nwhere 0.0 is identical and 1.0 is orthogonal) between the\nnormalized caption and the pseudo-label, filtering out labels\nwith a distance above a threshold. This allows us to treat the\nhuman-generated labels in captions as ground truth without\nintroducing excessive noise. After filtering out noisy labels,\nwe prepare the remaining text by applying standardized\npunctuation and capitalization.\nPreprocessing unlabeled speech. The majority of speech\navailable on the web is unlabeled. In these cases, we lever-\nage the Whisper large v3 model to generate training labels\nfor our lighter-weight Moonshine model. The risk inherent\nin training one model on another model’s outputs is that\nthe new model learns the old model’s errors. From inspec-\ntion, we noted that the majority of hallucinated outputs from\nWhisper large v3 occurred below a predictable value of the\naverage log probability of the output. We thus mitigate\nthe risk of introducing hallucination and other noise in the\ntraining set by filtering out instances with an average log\nprobability below this threshold. During this process, we\nbenefited from speed-ups provided by batched inference in\nthe WhisperX implementation (Bain et al., 2023).\nControlling instance duration. We assemble successive\nspeech segments into longer training instances, such that\nthe resulting instance duration is ∈[4, 30] seconds with no\nmore than 2 seconds between successive segments. For\nour manually-captioned data sources, we use timestamped\nsegments provided by a subtitle file (e.g., an .srt file); for\nour pseudo-labeled audio, we use the timestamps output by\nWhisper. Upon combining this data with open datasets, the\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\nMoonshine: Speech Recognition for Live Transcription and Voice Commands\n4\nRoPE\nself-attention\nFFN + GELU\nFFN + SwiGLU\nRoPE\nself-attention\ncross-attention\n...\nConv1D\ntanh\nkernel size: 127\nstride: 64\nConv1D\nGELU\nkernel size: 7\nstride: 3\n...\nConv1D\nkernel size: 3\nstride: 2\nEncoder\nBlocks\nDecoder\nBlocks\nnext-token\nprediction\nBPE\nGELU\nFigure 3. Training\nWe performed training on a 32x H100 GPU cluster, using\nGPU data parallelism provided by Huggingface’s Accel-\nerate library (Gugger et al., 2022). We used Accelerate’s\nBF16 mixed precision optimizations. We trained the mod-\nels for 250K steps with a batch size of 32 per GPU (1024\nglobal batch size). WER comparison of base Moonshine vs. Whisper models\n4. Evaluations\nOur experiments measure Moonshine against OpenAI’s\nWhisper models, specifically Whisper tiny.en and\nbase.en. These two are the most suitable for running\non low cost edge devices given their lower memory and\ncompute requirements. We measure the WER on datasets in\nthe OpenASR Leaderboard (Srivastav et al., 2023). We use\ngreedy decoding, with a heuristic limit of 6 output tokens\nper second of audio to avoid repeated output sequences.\nMoonshine Tiny and Base models achieve better average\nWER compared to their Whisper counterparts (tiny.en\nand base.en, respectively), as depicted in Table 2 and\nTable 3. Earnings22 is the dataset for which Moonshine per-\nforms the worst compared to Whisper. 8% of the examples\nin Earnings22 are less than 1 second long, and the corre-\nsponding transcriptions are short utterances such as “So.”,\n“Yes.”, “Okay.”, etc. Moonshine models tend to produce\nrepeated tokens in the output for such examples, leading to\nWhisper\ntiny.en\nMoonshine\nTiny\nAMI\n24.24\n22.77\nEarnings22\n19.12\n21.25\nGigaspeech\n14.08\n14.41\nLibreSpeech (Clean)\n5.66\n4.52\nLibreSpeech (Other)\n15.45\n11.71\nSPGISpeech\n5.93\n7.7\nTEDLium\n5.97\n5.64\nVoxpopuli\n12.00\n13.27\nAverage\n12.81\n12.66\nTable 3. WER comparison of tiny Moonshine vs. Whisper models\na WER greater than 100%. Fewer than 0.5% of examples in\nour training set are shorter than a second. We hypothesize\nthat this weakens generalization to the Earnings22 dataset.\n4.1. Accuracy vs. Input Length\nMoonshine models demonstrate the ability to transcribe\naudio clips of varying lengths. However, we hypothesize\nthat shorter inputs may result in higher WER due to the lack\nof sufficient contextual information. Conversely, for longer\nclips—particularly those exceeding the sequence lengths\nobserved during training—there is a notable increase in\ntranscription errors due to hallucinations. To evaluate this\nbehavior, we measured the WER across a range of audio\nsegments from the TEDLium dataset, with clip durations\nvarying between 10 and 55 seconds.\nTo evaluate inference speed across different clip lengths,\nwe compare Moonshine and Whisper on GPU (H100). The\nresults are depicted in Figure 5. Robustness comparisons on LibriSpeech test-clean. Left: WER as linear gain of test audio increases. Moonshine degrades\nmore at the lowest gains, but maintains superior WER values at -40 dB and beyond. Right: WER as signal-to-noise ratio increases under\nadditive computer fan noise. Moonshine degrades similarly to OpenAI’s Whisper counterpart while maintaining superior WER values.\nis limited to clips up to Whisper’s 30-second maximum,\nas longer clips require multiple Whisper runs, introducing\noverhead and skewing the results unfairly against Whisper.\nMoonshine demonstrates superior performance, particularly\non shorter sequences, due to its support for variable se-\nquence lengths in the encoder.\n4.2. Robustness to Input Speech Signal Level\nDuring this work, we observed that the robustness of Whis-\nper models varies relative to the level of the input speech\nsignal. To measure the impact of input levels (i.e., linear\ngain) on Moonshine, we tested model robustness as increas-\ning gain is applied to the audio samples in the dataset. As\nshown in the left portion of Figure 6, Moonshine Base main-\ntains superior WER over most of this range until the input\nspeech signal is very attenuated. At this point (where gain\nis below -40 dB, i.e., the input audio is very quiet) the WER\nrises above the Whisper model. For practical applications\nwhere high speech attenuation can be avoided in system\ndesign, Moonshine Base is robust to varying input levels.\n4.3. Robustness to Additive Noise\nWe tested the noise robustness of the Moonshine Base model\nby measuring the WER for the fan noise observed in a tablet\ncomputer application under load. In user studies, we quanti-\nfied the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) for this application in\nthe range [9, 17] dB depending on the speaker. We calculate\nthe level of additive noise corresponding to a given SNR\nbased on the average signal power of individual dataset ex-\namples with quiet sections removed. The right portion of\nFigure 6 shows how the performance degrades as fan noise\nincreases. Whisper’s robustness to noise is known (Radford\net al., 2022); Moonshine Base maintains this robustness\nwhile providing a superior WER.\n5. Discussion & Conclusion\nWe briefly discuss limitations before concluding the paper\nin this section.\nArchitectures and optimizers.\nAs in any research of\nthis kind, many variations of model architecture could be\nexplored. Likewise, recent advancements in optimizers—\nparticularly Shampoo (Gupta et al., 2018) and SOAP (Vyas\net al., 2024)—show promise for improving WERs in our\narchitecture. Conducting an ablation study on these model\narchitectures and training methods would significantly en-\nhance the community’s understanding of the models’ limi-\ntations. However, due to resource constraints, particularly\nlimited affordability of GPUs, such studies are beyond the\nscope of this paper. Consequently, we chose our architec-\nture and optimizer based on the authors’ experience and a\nthorough review of relevant literature.\nGeneralization to shorter audio segments. The relatively\nlow performance that we observed on the Earnings22 dataset\nmotivates collection of more data representative of this case,\nand investigation of training techniques to introduce more\ncontextual information in the model. This will likely im-\nprove model performance with no changes to architecture.\nIn this paper we introduced Moonshine, a family of\nlightweight ASR models optimized for low latency, on-\ndevice speech-to-text applications. We outlined our model\narchitecture, data collection and preprocessing procedures,\nand training. We benchmarked Moonshine against two\nOpenAI Whisper models—tiny.en and base.en—\nshowing that their Moonshine counterparts provide up to 3x\nreductions in latency scaled to the duration of input audio.\nOur work opens the door for new applications of real-time\nASR in live transcription, accessibility technologies, and\nsmart devices.\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nMoonshine: Speech Recognition for Live Transcription and Voice Commands\n7\n6.
